I’m here because… honestly, it’s been getting hard to keep pretending this is just normal work stuff. I work in transportation, and dispatch and compliance have been watching my route changes through the computer system. Like, when I adjust something or take a different turn, it’s like they already know and then there’ll be these little comments on the radio—not direct enough that someone else would catch it, maybe, but I can tell it’s about me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know… I wouldn’t say it like that exactly. I mean, at work there’s always noise—radios, engines, doors, people talking over each other—so sometimes I’ll hear something and not be sure where it came from. But when you say “voices with no radio or obvious source,” I’m not sure I’ve really had that in a clear way.

It’s more that I’ll notice comments or sounds and then I look around and other people act like they didn’t hear it or they pretend they didn’t. So it’s hard for me to answer, because I don’t always know if they truly didn’t hear it, or if they’re just acting like it wasn’t said. But no, I don’t think I’ve had, like, a voice in an empty room talking to me, if that’s what you mean.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… if I’m being completely honest, like 85, maybe 90 percent sure. I know that sounds high, and I know people will say, “Well, dispatch has to know where you are, that’s their job,” and yes, I get that. I’m not saying the whole system shouldn’t exist or that nobody else gets tracked. We all have routes and logs and times and everything goes through the computer.

But the part that makes me so sure is how it lines up. If I change something on my route, or if I’m thinking I’m going to avoid a certain street, then suddenly there’s a radio comment that’s not saying my name, but it’s worded in this way where I know it’s about me. Or another driver will be in just the right place, like they’re checking whether I did what I was supposed to do. And then later someone says something casual that connects back to it.

So… yeah, I leave a little room because I’m trying to be reasonable, and I know I can’t prove every single piece of it. But it doesn’t feel like a coincidence. It feels like dispatch and compliance are watching the route changes and certain drivers are feeding things back. So I’d say around 90 percent.

I’m not stupid. But this feels different. It’s the way certain drivers will say things or suddenly be around, and I’m pretty sure some of them are reporting on me. I just… I need someone to take it seriously, because it’s making it really hard to do my job like normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or sounds that other people around you don’t seem to hear, when there’s no radio or other obvious source?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say it like that exactly. I mean, at work there’s always noise—radios, engines, doors, people talking over each other—so sometimes I’ll hear something and not be sure where it came from. But when you say “voices with no radio or obvious source,” I’m not sure I’ve really had that in a clear way.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll notice comments or sounds and then I look around and other people act like they didn’t hear it or they pretend they didn’t. So it’s hard for me to answer, because I don’t always know if they truly didn’t hear it, or if they’re just acting like it wasn’t said. But no, I don’t think I’ve had, like, a voice in an empty room talking to me, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: When you think about dispatch and the other drivers watching and commenting on you, how sure are you that this is really happening, on a scale from 0 to 100 percent?\n\nPatient: I mean… if I’m being completely honest, like 85, maybe 90 percent sure. I know that sounds high, and I know people will say, “Well, dispatch has to know where you are, that’s their job,” and yes, I get that. I’m not saying the whole system shouldn’t exist or that nobody else gets tracked. We all have routes and logs and times and everything goes through the computer.\n\nBut the part that makes me so sure is how it lines up. If I change something on my route, or if I’m thinking I’m going to avoid a certain street, then suddenly there’s a radio comment that’s not saying my name, but it’s worded in this way where I know it’s about me. Or another driver will be in just the right place, like they’re checking whether I did what I was supposed to do. And then later someone says something casual that connects back to it.\n\nSo… yeah, I leave a little room because I’m trying to be reasonable, and I know I can’t prove every single piece of it. But it doesn’t feel like a coincidence. It feels like dispatch and compliance are watching the route changes and certain drivers are feeding things back. So I’d say around 90 percent.\n\n[Last Question]\nIn the past few weeks, have you been feeling noticeably down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / transportation_worker / associates\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not “most of the day, nearly every day,” no. I mean, I’ve been stressed and worn down, definitely, and there are days where I come home and I just feel heavy because I’m replaying everything that happened at work and wondering who said what or what it meant. But if you mean depressed like just sad all day, every day, I don’t know that I’d call it that.

It’s more like I’m tense and frustrated, and sometimes exhausted from trying to stay on top of things. There are moments where I feel pretty low, especially when I feel like no one believes me or they brush it off like I’m overreacting. But it’s not like I wake up every single day feeling depressed from morning to night. At least, I don’t think that’s the main thing.

You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I can consider it for a minute, especially if it’s someone I trust and they’re not saying it in a mocking way. Like, I’ll try to step back and tell myself, okay, maybe dispatch is just doing what dispatch does, maybe compliance is just looking at the computer because that’s normal for routes and timing and all that. I’m not unreasonable—I know there’s oversight in transportation, and I know not every radio comment is automatically about me.

But then when I go back over the details, it stops feeling like coincidence. It’s the pattern of it. Like if I change a route or I do something slightly different, and then there’s a comment on the radio that’s phrased just vaguely enough, but it fits exactly with what I did. Or certain drivers show up in places where it feels like they’re checking on whether I followed the route. And then later someone says something that connects to that same thing, like they already heard about it.

So yes, I can briefly consider that maybe I’m misunderstanding some of it. I do try. But it doesn’t really stay that way in my head, because it feels too specific. Even when people say, “That’s just normal oversight,” I still feel pretty sure that certain drivers are reporting back and that dispatch or compliance are tracking more than they admit.

There are moments where I feel pretty low, especially when I feel like no one believes me or they brush it off like I’m overreacting. But it’s not like I wake up every single day feeling depressed from morning to night. At least, I don’t think that’s the main thing.\n\nDoctor: When someone you trust suggests that these things at work might be coincidences or misunderstandings, are you ever able to seriously consider that possibility, even briefly, or does it still feel definitely true to you no matter what they say?\n\nPatient: I can consider it for a minute, especially if it’s someone I trust and they’re not saying it in a mocking way. Not really in the same way, no. I mean… I’m more on edge in public now, so if I’m at a store or in my building and somebody looks at me too long, I might notice it more than I used to. But I don’t have the same sense that the neighbors or random people at the grocery store are sending me messages.

The main thing is work. It’s dispatch and compliance, because they actually have the computer system and the route information, so that makes sense to me. They can see when I change something or when I’m not exactly where I’m expected to be, and then there’ll be those little comments on the radio that don’t say my name but feel like they’re aimed at me. And certain drivers being around at certain times, like they’re checking or reporting back—that’s what I keep seeing.

So outside of work, I might be more alert, I guess, but it doesn’t feel organized like that. It’s really tied to the job and the route tracking and the drivers.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 10 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n6\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23, F20.0, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… honestly, it’s been getting hard to keep pretending this is just normal work stuff. I work in transportation, and dispatch and compliance have been watching my route changes through the computer system. Like, when I adjust something or take a different turn, it’s like they already know and then there’ll be these little comments on the radio—not direct enough that someone else would catch it, maybe, but I can tell it’s about me.\n\nPeople keep saying, “That’s just oversight,” or “They track everyone,” and I understand that there’s a system for routes and schedules and all that. I’m not stupid. But this feels different. It’s the way certain drivers will say things or suddenly be around, and I’m pretty sure some of them are reporting on me. I just… I need someone to take it seriously, because it’s making it really hard to do my job like normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or sounds that other people around you don’t seem to hear, when there’s no radio or other obvious source?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say it like that exactly. I mean, at work there’s always noise—radios, engines, doors, people talking over each other—so sometimes I’ll hear something and not be sure where it came from. But when you say “voices with no radio or obvious source,” I’m not sure I’ve really had that in a clear way.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll notice comments or sounds and then I look around and other people act like they didn’t hear it or they pretend they didn’t. So it’s hard for me to answer, because I don’t always know if they truly didn’t hear it, or if they’re just acting like it wasn’t said. But no, I don’t think I’ve had, like, a voice in an empty room talking to me, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: When you think about dispatch and the other drivers watching and commenting on you, how sure are you that this is really happening, on a scale from 0 to 100 percent?\n\nPatient: I mean… if I’m being completely honest, like 85, maybe 90 percent sure. I know that sounds high, and I know people will say, “Well, dispatch has to know where you are, that’s their job,” and yes, I get that. I’m not saying the whole system shouldn’t exist or that nobody else gets tracked. We all have routes and logs and times and everything goes through the computer.\n\nBut the part that makes me so sure is how it lines up. If I change something on my route, or if I’m thinking I’m going to avoid a certain street, then suddenly there’s a radio comment that’s not saying my name, but it’s worded in this way where I know it’s about me. Or another driver will be in just the right place, like they’re checking whether I did what I was supposed to do. And then later someone says something casual that connects back to it.\n\nSo… yeah, I leave a little room because I’m trying to be reasonable, and I know I can’t prove every single piece of it. But it doesn’t feel like a coincidence. It feels like dispatch and compliance are watching the route changes and certain drivers are feeding things back. So I’d say around 90 percent.\n\nDoctor: In the past few weeks, have you been feeling noticeably down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not “most of the day, nearly every day,” no. I mean, I’ve been stressed and worn down, definitely, and there are days where I come home and I just feel heavy because I’m replaying everything that happened at work and wondering who said what or what it meant. But if you mean depressed like just sad all day, every day, I don’t know that I’d call it that.\n\nIt’s more like I’m tense and frustrated, and sometimes exhausted from trying to stay on top of things. There are moments where I feel pretty low, especially when I feel like no one believes me or they brush it off like I’m overreacting. But it’s not like I wake up every single day feeling depressed from morning to night. At least, I don’t think that’s the main thing.\n\nDoctor: When someone you trust suggests that these things at work might be coincidences or misunderstandings, are you ever able to seriously consider that possibility, even briefly, or does it still feel definitely true to you no matter what they say?\n\nPatient: I can consider it for a minute, especially if it’s someone I trust and they’re not saying it in a mocking way. Like, I’ll try to step back and tell myself, okay, maybe dispatch is just doing what dispatch does, maybe compliance is just looking at the computer because that’s normal for routes and timing and all that. I’m not unreasonable—I know there’s oversight in transportation, and I know not every radio comment is automatically about me.\n\nBut then when I go back over the details, it stops feeling like coincidence. It’s the pattern of it. Like if I change a route or I do something slightly different, and then there’s a comment on the radio that’s phrased just vaguely enough, but it fits exactly with what I did. Or certain drivers show up in places where it feels like they’re checking on whether I followed the route. And then later someone says something that connects to that same thing, like they already heard about it.\n\nSo yes, I can briefly consider that maybe I’m misunderstanding some of it. I do try. But it doesn’t really stay that way in my head, because it feels too specific. Even when people say, “That’s just normal oversight,” I still feel pretty sure that certain drivers are reporting back and that dispatch or compliance are tracking more than they admit.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your job, do you ever feel that people in other places—like neighbors, stores, or in public—are also watching you or sending you hidden messages about you?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean… I’m more on edge in public now, so if I’m at a store or in my building and somebody looks at me too long, I might notice it more than I used to. But I don’t have the same sense that the neighbors or random people at the grocery store are sending me messages.\n\nThe main thing is work. It’s dispatch and compliance, because they actually have the computer system and the route information, so that makes sense to me. Yeah… I mean, not always in a dramatic way like refusing everything, but yes, it has changed how I’m functioning. At work I’ve fallen behind on the route reports, which is embarrassing because I used to be pretty on top of those. I’ll sit there trying to write it up and then I start second-guessing what to include, or whether something I write is going to be used against me or picked apart, and then it just doesn’t get done the way it should.

And my instructions to drivers have gotten… I guess confusing. I don’t mean to make them confusing, but I’ll be trying to be careful with how I word things, or I’ll change how I explain a route because I’m thinking about who might be listening or what they’re going to say about it later. Then the drivers complain because they’re like, “What exactly are you telling me to do?” or they say I wasn’t clear. And then that just makes me feel worse, because it feels like it gives people more reason to watch me.

At home it’s been showing up too. My husband has had to take over a lot of the normal stuff—meals, phone calls, basic planning things—because I’m not keeping up with it. Like I’ll mean to call someone back or figure out dinner or handle some simple errand, and then I get stuck thinking through what happened at work or trying to sort out what it all means, and hours go by. So yes, I guess I have changed, or things have changed around me because I’m not managing things the same way.
=====================================

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… honestly, it’s been getting hard to keep pretending this is just normal work stuff. I work in transportation, and dispatch and compliance have been watching my route changes through the computer system. Like, when I adjust something or take a different turn, it’s like they already know and then there’ll be these little comments on the radio—not direct enough that someone else would catch it, maybe, but I can tell it’s about me.\n\nPeople keep saying, “That’s just oversight,” or “They track everyone,” and I understand that there’s a system for routes and schedules and all that. I’m not stupid. But this feels different. It’s the way certain drivers will say things or suddenly be around, and I’m pretty sure some of them are reporting on me. I just… I need someone to take it seriously, because it’s making it really hard to do my job like normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or sounds that other people around you don’t seem to hear, when there’s no radio or other obvious source?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say it like that exactly. I mean, at work there’s always noise—radios, engines, doors, people talking over each other—so sometimes I’ll hear something and not be sure where it came from. But when you say “voices with no radio or obvious source,” I’m not sure I’ve really had that in a clear way.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll notice comments or sounds and then I look around and other people act like they didn’t hear it or they pretend they didn’t. So it’s hard for me to answer, because I don’t always know if they truly didn’t hear it, or if they’re just acting like it wasn’t said. But no, I don’t think I’ve had, like, a voice in an empty room talking to me, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: When you think about dispatch and the other drivers watching and commenting on you, how sure are you that this is really happening, on a scale from 0 to 100 percent?\n\nPatient: I mean… if I’m being completely honest, like 85, maybe 90 percent sure. I know that sounds high, and I know people will say, “Well, dispatch has to know where you are, that’s their job,” and yes, I get that. I’m not saying the whole system shouldn’t exist or that nobody else gets tracked. We all have routes and logs and times and everything goes through the computer.\n\nBut the part that makes me so sure is how it lines up. If I change something on my route, or if I’m thinking I’m going to avoid a certain street, then suddenly there’s a radio comment that’s not saying my name, but it’s worded in this way where I know it’s about me. Or another driver will be in just the right place, like they’re checking whether I did what I was supposed to do. And then later someone says something casual that connects back to it.\n\nSo… yeah, I leave a little room because I’m trying to be reasonable, and I know I can’t prove every single piece of it. But it doesn’t feel like a coincidence. It feels like dispatch and compliance are watching the route changes and certain drivers are feeding things back. So I’d say around 90 percent.\n\nDoctor: In the past few weeks, have you been feeling noticeably down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not “most of the day, nearly every day,” no. I mean, I’ve been stressed and worn down, definitely, and there are days where I come home and I just feel heavy because I’m replaying everything that happened at work and wondering who said what or what it meant. But if you mean depressed like just sad all day, every day, I don’t know that I’d call it that.\n\nIt’s more like I’m tense and frustrated, and sometimes exhausted from trying to stay on top of things. There are moments where I feel pretty low, especially when I feel like no one believes me or they brush it off like I’m overreacting. But it’s not like I wake up every single day feeling depressed from morning to night. At least, I don’t think that’s the main thing.\n\nDoctor: When someone you trust suggests that these things at work might be coincidences or misunderstandings, are you ever able to seriously consider that possibility, even briefly, or does it still feel definitely true to you no matter what they say?\n\nPatient: I can consider it for a minute, especially if it’s someone I trust and they’re not saying it in a mocking way. Like, I’ll try to step back and tell myself, okay, maybe dispatch is just doing what dispatch does, maybe compliance is just looking at the computer because that’s normal for routes and timing and all that. I’m not unreasonable—I know there’s oversight in transportation, and I know not every radio comment is automatically about me.\n\nBut then when I go back over the details, it stops feeling like coincidence. It’s the pattern of it. Like if I change a route or I do something slightly different, and then there’s a comment on the radio that’s phrased just vaguely enough, but it fits exactly with what I did. Or certain drivers show up in places where it feels like they’re checking on whether I followed the route. And then later someone says something that connects to that same thing, like they already heard about it.\n\nSo yes, I can briefly consider that maybe I’m misunderstanding some of it. I do try. But it doesn’t really stay that way in my head, because it feels too specific. Even when people say, “That’s just normal oversight,” I still feel pretty sure that certain drivers are reporting back and that dispatch or compliance are tracking more than they admit.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your job, do you ever feel that people in other places—like neighbors, stores, or in public—are also watching you or sending you hidden messages about you?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean… I’m more on edge in public now, so if I’m at a store or in my building and somebody looks at me too long, I might notice it more than I used to. But I don’t have the same sense that the neighbors or random people at the grocery store are sending me messages.\n\nThe main thing is work. It’s dispatch and compliance, because they actually have the computer system and the route information, so that makes sense to me. They can see when I change something or when I’m not exactly where I’m expected to be, and then there’ll be those little comments on the radio that don’t say my name but feel like they’re aimed at me. And certain drivers being around at certain times, like they’re checking or reporting back—that’s what I keep seeing.\n\nSo outside of work, I might be more alert, I guess, but it doesn’t feel organized like that. It’s really tied to the job and the route tracking and the drivers.\n\nDoctor: Because of these concerns about dispatch and the other drivers, have you actually changed your behavior in any clear way—like avoiding certain routes, refusing assignments, or confronting people about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not always in a dramatic way like refusing everything, but yes, it has changed how I’m functioning. Even when people say, “That’s just normal oversight,” I still feel pretty sure that certain drivers are reporting back and that dispatch or compliance are tracking more than they admit.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your job, do you ever feel that people in other places—like neighbors, stores, or in public—are also watching you or sending you hidden messages about you?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean… I’m more on edge in public now, so if I’m at a store or in my building and somebody looks at me too long, I might notice it more than I used to. But I don’t have the same sense that the neighbors or random people at the grocery store are sending me messages.\n\nThe main thing is work. It’s dispatch and compliance, because they actually have the computer system and the route information, so that makes sense to me. They can see when I change something or when I’m not exactly where I’m expected to be, and then there’ll be those little comments on the radio that don’t say my name but feel like they’re aimed at me. And certain drivers being around at certain times, like they’re checking or reporting back—that’s what I keep seeing.\n\nSo outside of work, I might be more alert, I guess, but it doesn’t feel organized like that. It’s really tied to the job and the route tracking and the drivers.\n\nDoctor: Because of these concerns about dispatch and the other drivers, have you actually changed your behavior in any clear way—like avoiding certain routes, refusing assignments, or confronting people about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not always in a dramatic way like refusing everything, but yes, it has changed how I’m functioning. At work I’ve fallen behind on the route reports, which is embarrassing because I used to be pretty on top of those. I’ll sit there trying to write it up and then I start second-guessing what to include, or whether something I write is going to be used against me or picked apart, and then it just doesn’t get done the way it should.\n\nAnd my instructions to drivers have gotten… I guess confusing. I don’t mean to make them confusing, but I’ll be trying to be careful with how I word things, or I’ll change how I explain a route because I’m thinking about who might be listening or what they’re going to say about it later. Then the drivers complain because they’re like, “What exactly are you telling me to do?” or they say I wasn’t clear. And then that just makes me feel worse, because it feels like it gives people more reason to watch me.\n\nAt home it’s been showing up too. My husband has had to take over a lot of the normal stuff—meals, phone calls, basic planning things—because I’m not keeping up with it. Like I’ll mean to call someone back or figure out dinner or handle some simple errand, and then I get stuck thinking through what happened at work or trying to sort out what it all means, and hours go by. So yes, I guess I have changed, or things have changed around me because I’m not managing things the same way.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you think specifically about the idea that dispatch and certain drivers are tracking and reporting on you, how distressing does that belief feel to you in the moment, on a scale from 0 to 10, where 0 is not upsetting at all and 10 is the most upset you can imagine?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / transportation_worker / associates\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because dispatch and compliance have been tracking my route changes through the computer system and little comments on the radio, and even when people say it’s just normal oversight, I can tell certain drivers are reporting on me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
In the moment? It’s pretty high. I’d say… probably an 8 out of 10 most of the time, sometimes a 9 if it’s happening right then, like if I’ve just made a route change and then there’s some comment over the radio that feels pointed, or I notice one of the same drivers nearby again.

It’s not just annoying, it feels like my stomach drops, like I’m being watched and evaluated and I can’t just do my job normally. Even when I try to tell myself, okay, maybe this is just dispatch doing normal oversight, it doesn’t calm me down for long, because then I start thinking about the computer system and how they can see the route changes, and how the comments are said in this indirect way, and how certain drivers seem to know things or report back.

So yeah… I wouldn’t say it’s a 10 all day every day, because I can still talk to you and I can still try to reason through it. But when it feels active, when I feel like they’re tracking me or checking up on me, it’s very upsetting. Around an 8.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23, F20.0, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… honestly, it’s been getting hard to keep pretending this is just normal work stuff. I work in transportation, and dispatch and compliance have been watching my route changes through the computer system. Like, when I adjust something or take a different turn, it’s like they already know and then there’ll be these little comments on the radio—not direct enough that someone else would catch it, maybe, but I can tell it’s about me.\n\nPeople keep saying, “That’s just oversight,” or “They track everyone,” and I understand that there’s a system for routes and schedules and all that. I’m not stupid. But this feels different. It’s the way certain drivers will say things or suddenly be around, and I’m pretty sure some of them are reporting on me. I just… I need someone to take it seriously, because it’s making it really hard to do my job like normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or sounds that other people around you don’t seem to hear, when there’s no radio or other obvious source?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say it like that exactly. I mean, at work there’s always noise—radios, engines, doors, people talking over each other—so sometimes I’ll hear something and not be sure where it came from. But when you say “voices with no radio or obvious source,” I’m not sure I’ve really had that in a clear way.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll notice comments or sounds and then I look around and other people act like they didn’t hear it or they pretend they didn’t. So it’s hard for me to answer, because I don’t always know if they truly didn’t hear it, or if they’re just acting like it wasn’t said. But no, I don’t think I’ve had, like, a voice in an empty room talking to me, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: When you think about dispatch and the other drivers watching and commenting on you, how sure are you that this is really happening, on a scale from 0 to 100 percent?\n\nPatient: I mean… if I’m being completely honest, like 85, maybe 90 percent sure. I know that sounds high, and I know people will say, “Well, dispatch has to know where you are, that’s their job,” and yes, I get that. I’m not saying the whole system shouldn’t exist or that nobody else gets tracked. We all have routes and logs and times and everything goes through the computer.\n\nBut the part that makes me so sure is how it lines up. If I change something on my route, or if I’m thinking I’m going to avoid a certain street, then suddenly there’s a radio comment that’s not saying my name, but it’s worded in this way where I know it’s about me. Or another driver will be in just the right place, like they’re checking whether I did what I was supposed to do. And then later someone says something casual that connects back to it.\n\nSo… yeah, I leave a little room because I’m trying to be reasonable, and I know I can’t prove every single piece of it. But it doesn’t feel like a coincidence. It feels like dispatch and compliance are watching the route changes and certain drivers are feeding things back. So I’d say around 90 percent.\n\nDoctor: In the past few weeks, have you been feeling noticeably down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not “most of the day, nearly every day,” no. I mean, I’ve been stressed and worn down, definitely, and there are days where I come home and I just feel heavy because I’m replaying everything that happened at work and wondering who said what or what it meant. But if you mean depressed like just sad all day, every day, I don’t know that I’d call it that.\n\nIt’s more like I’m tense and frustrated, and sometimes exhausted from trying to stay on top of things. There are moments where I feel pretty low, especially when I feel like no one believes me or they brush it off like I’m overreacting. But it’s not like I wake up every single day feeling depressed from morning to night. At least, I don’t think that’s the main thing.\n\nDoctor: When someone you trust suggests that these things at work might be coincidences or misunderstandings, are you ever able to seriously consider that possibility, even briefly, or does it still feel definitely true to you no matter what they say?\n\nPatient: I can consider it for a minute, especially if it’s someone I trust and they’re not saying it in a mocking way. Like, I’ll try to step back and tell myself, okay, maybe dispatch is just doing what dispatch does, maybe compliance is just looking at the computer because that’s normal for routes and timing and all that. I’m not unreasonable—I know there’s oversight in transportation, and I know not every radio comment is automatically about me.\n\nBut then when I go back over the details, it stops feeling like coincidence. It’s the pattern of it. Like if I change a route or I do something slightly different, and then there’s a comment on the radio that’s phrased just vaguely enough, but it fits exactly with what I did. Or certain drivers show up in places where it feels like they’re checking on whether I followed the route. And then later someone says something that connects to that same thing, like they already heard about it.\n\nSo yes, I can briefly consider that maybe I’m misunderstanding some of it. I do try. But it doesn’t really stay that way in my head, because it feels too specific. Even when people say, “That’s just normal oversight,” I still feel pretty sure that certain drivers are reporting back and that dispatch or compliance are tracking more than they admit.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your job, do you ever feel that people in other places—like neighbors, stores, or in public—are also watching you or sending you hidden messages about you?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean… I’m more on edge in public now, so if I’m at a store or in my building and somebody looks at me too long, I might notice it more than I used to. But I don’t have the same sense that the neighbors or random people at the grocery store are sending me messages.\n\nThe main thing is work. It’s dispatch and compliance, because they actually have the computer system and the route information, so that makes sense to me. They can see when I change something or when I’m not exactly where I’m expected to be, and then there’ll be those little comments on the radio that don’t say my name but feel like they’re aimed at me. And certain drivers being around at certain times, like they’re checking or reporting back—that’s what I keep seeing.\n\nSo outside of work, I might be more alert, I guess, but it doesn’t feel organized like that. It’s really tied to the job and the route tracking and the drivers.\n\nDoctor: Because of these concerns about dispatch and the other drivers, have you actually changed your behavior in any clear way—like avoiding certain routes, refusing assignments, or confronting people about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not always in a dramatic way like refusing everything, but yes, it has changed how I’m functioning. I’ll sit there trying to write it up and then I start second-guessing what to include, or whether something I write is going to be used against me or picked apart, and then it just doesn’t get done the way it should.\n\nAnd my instructions to drivers have gotten… I guess confusing. Then the drivers complain because they’re like, “What exactly are you telling me to do?” or they say I wasn’t clear. felt clear to me for about the past 2 months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / transportation_worker / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S019\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Outside of your job, do you ever feel that people in other places—like neighbors, stores, or in public—are also watching you or sending you hidden messages about you?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean… I’m more on edge in public now, so if I’m at a store or in my building and somebody looks at me too long, I might notice it more than I used to. But I don’t have the same sense that the neighbors or random people at the grocery store are sending me messages.\n\nThe main thing is work. It’s dispatch and compliance, because they actually have the computer system and the route information, so that makes sense to me. They can see when I change something or when I’m not exactly where I’m expected to be, and then there’ll be those little comments on the radio that don’t say my name but feel like they’re aimed at me. And certain drivers being around at certain times, like they’re checking or reporting back—that’s what I keep seeing.\n\nSo outside of work, I might be more alert, I guess, but it doesn’t feel organized like that. It’s really tied to the job and the route tracking and the drivers.\n\nDoctor: Because of these concerns about dispatch and the other drivers, have you actually changed your behavior in any clear way—like avoiding certain routes, refusing assignments, or confronting people about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not always in a dramatic way like refusing everything, but yes, it has changed how I’m functioning. At work I’ve fallen behind on the route reports, which is embarrassing because I used to be pretty on top of those. I’ll sit there trying to write it up and then I start second-guessing what to include, or whether something I write is going to be used against me or picked apart, and then it just doesn’t get done the way it should.\n\nAnd my instructions to drivers have gotten… I guess confusing. I don’t mean to make them confusing, but I’ll be trying to be careful with how I word things, or I’ll change how I explain a route because I’m thinking about who might be listening or what they’re going to say about it later. Then the drivers complain because they’re like, “What exactly are you telling me to do?” or they say I wasn’t clear. And then that just makes me feel worse, because it feels like it gives people more reason to watch me.\n\nAt home it’s been showing up too. My husband has had to take over a lot of the normal stuff—meals, phone calls, basic planning things—because I’m not keeping up with it. Like I’ll mean to call someone back or figure out dinner or handle some simple errand, and then I get stuck thinking through what happened at work or trying to sort out what it all means, and hours go by. So yes, I guess I have changed, or things have changed around me because I’m not managing things the same way.\n\nDoctor: When you think specifically about the idea that dispatch and certain drivers are tracking and reporting on you, how distressing does that belief feel to you in the moment, on a scale from 0 to 10, where 0 is not upsetting at all and 10 is the most upset you can imagine?\n\nPatient: In the moment? It’s pretty high. I’d say… probably an 8 out of 10 most of the time, sometimes a 9 if it’s happening right then, like if I’ve just made a route change and then there’s some comment over the radio that feels pointed, or I notice one of the same drivers nearby again.\n\nIt’s not just annoying, it feels like my stomach drops, like I’m being watched and evaluated and I can’t just do my job normally. Even when I try to tell myself, okay, maybe this is just dispatch doing normal oversight, it doesn’t calm me down for long, because then I start thinking about the computer system and how they can see the route changes, and how the comments are said in this indirect way, and how certain drivers seem to know things or report back.\n\nSo yeah… I wouldn’t say it’s a 10 all day every day, because I can still talk to you and I can still try to reason through it. But when it feels active, when I feel like they’re tracking me or checking up on me, it’s very upsetting. Around an 8.\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore this situation at work started, have you ever had a period in your life when you were clearly out of touch with reality—like being convinced of something others said was impossible, or needing hospital care because of your thoughts or perceptions?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… honestly, it’s been getting hard to keep pretending this is just normal work stuff. I work in transportation, and dispatch and compliance have been watching my route changes through the computer system. Like, when I adjust something or take a different turn, it’s like they already know and then there’ll be these little comments on the radio—not direct enough that someone else would catch it, maybe, but I can tell it’s about me.\n\nPeople keep saying, “That’s just oversight,” or “They track everyone,” and I understand that there’s a system for routes and schedules and all that. I’m not stupid. But this feels different. It’s the way certain drivers will say things or suddenly be around, and I’m pretty sure some of them are reporting on me. I just… I need someone to take it seriously, because it’s making it really hard to do my job like normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or sounds that other people around you don’t seem to hear, when there’s no radio or other obvious source?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say it like that exactly. I mean, at work there’s always noise—radios, engines, doors, people talking over each other—so sometimes I’ll hear something and not be sure where it came from. But when you say “voices with no radio or obvious source,” I’m not sure I’ve really had that in a clear way.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll notice comments or sounds and then I look around and other people act like they didn’t hear it or they pretend they didn’t. So it’s hard for me to answer, because I don’t always know if they truly didn’t hear it, or if they’re just acting like it wasn’t said. But no, I don’t think I’ve had, like, a voice in an empty room talking to me, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: When you think about dispatch and the other drivers watching and commenting on you, how sure are you that this is really happening, on a scale from 0 to 100 percent?\n\nPatient: I mean… if I’m being completely honest, like 85, maybe 90 percent sure. I know that sounds high, and I know people will say, “Well, dispatch has to know where you are, that’s their job,” and yes, I get that. I’m not saying the whole system shouldn’t exist or that nobody else gets tracked. We all have routes and logs and times and everything goes through the computer.\n\nBut the part that makes me so sure is how it lines up. If I change something on my route, or if I’m thinking I’m going to avoid a certain street, then suddenly there’s a radio comment that’s not saying my name, but it’s worded in this way where I know it’s about me. Or another driver will be in just the right place, like they’re checking whether I did what I was supposed to do. And then later someone says something casual that connects back to it.\n\nSo… yeah, I leave a little room because I’m trying to be reasonable, and I know I can’t prove every single piece of it. But it doesn’t feel like a coincidence. It feels like dispatch and compliance are watching the route changes and certain drivers are feeding things back. So I’d say around 90 percent.\n\nDoctor: In the past few weeks, have you been feeling noticeably down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not “most of the day, nearly every day,” no. I mean, I’ve been stressed and worn down, definitely, and there are days where I come home and I just feel heavy because I’m replaying everything that happened at work and wondering who said what or what it meant. But if you mean depressed like just sad all day, every day, I don’t know that I’d call it that.\n\nIt’s more like I’m tense and frustrated, and sometimes exhausted from trying to stay on top of things. There are moments where I feel pretty low, especially when I feel like no one believes me or they brush it off like I’m overreacting. But it’s not like I wake up every single day feeling depressed from morning to night. At least, I don’t think that’s the main thing.\n\nDoctor: When someone you trust suggests that these things at work might be coincidences or misunderstandings, are you ever able to seriously consider that possibility, even briefly, or does it still feel definitely true to you no matter what they say?\n\nPatient: I can consider it for a minute, especially if it’s someone I trust and they’re not saying it in a mocking way. Like, I’ll try to step back and tell myself, okay, maybe dispatch is just doing what dispatch does, maybe compliance is just looking at the computer because that’s normal for routes and timing and all that. I’m not unreasonable—I know there’s oversight in transportation, and I know not every radio comment is automatically about me.\n\nBut then when I go back over the details, it stops feeling like coincidence. It’s the pattern of it. Like if I change a route or I do something slightly different, and then there’s a comment on the radio that’s phrased just vaguely enough, but it fits exactly with what I did. Or certain drivers show up in places where it feels like they’re checking on whether I followed the route. And then later someone says something that connects to that same thing, like they already heard about it.\n\nSo yes, I can briefly consider that maybe I’m misunderstanding some of it. I do try. But it doesn’t really stay that way in my head, because it feels too specific. Even when people say, “That’s just normal oversight,” I still feel pretty sure that certain drivers are reporting back and that dispatch or compliance are tracking more than they admit.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your job, do you ever feel that people in other places—like neighbors, stores, or in public—are also watching you or sending you hidden messages about you?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean… I’m more on edge in public now, so if I’m at a store or in my building and somebody looks at me too long, I might notice it more than I used to. But I don’t have the same sense that the neighbors or random people at the grocery store are sending me messages.\n\nThe main thing is work. It’s dispatch and compliance, because they actually have the computer system and the route information, so that makes sense to me. They can see when I change something or when I’m not exactly where I’m expected to be, and then there’ll be those little comments on the radio that don’t say my name but feel like they’re aimed at me. And certain drivers being around at certain times, like they’re checking or reporting back—that’s what I keep seeing.\n\nSo outside of work, I might be more alert, I guess, but it doesn’t feel organized like that. It’s really tied to the job and the route tracking and the drivers.\n\nDoctor: Because of these concerns about dispatch and the other drivers, have you actually changed your behavior in any clear way—like avoiding certain routes, refusing assignments, or confronting people about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not always in a dramatic way like refusing everything, but yes, it has changed how I’m functioning. At work I’ve fallen behind on the route reports, which is embarrassing because I used to be pretty on top of those. I’ll sit there trying to write it up and then I start second-guessing what to include, or whether something I write is going to be used against me or picked apart, and then it just doesn’t get done the way it should.\n\nAnd my instructions to drivers have gotten… I guess confusing. I don’t mean to make them confusing, but I’ll be trying to be careful with how I word things, or I’ll change how I explain a route because I’m thinking about who might be listening or what they’re going to say about it later. Then the drivers complain because they’re like, “What exactly are you telling me to do?” or they say I wasn’t clear. And then that just makes me feel worse, because it feels like it gives people more reason to watch me.\n\nAt home it’s been showing up too. My husband has had to take over a lot of the normal stuff—meals, phone calls, basic planning things—because I’m not keeping up with it. Like I’ll mean to call someone back or figure out dinner or handle some simple errand, and then I get stuck thinking through what happened at work or trying to sort out what it all means, and hours go by. So yes, I guess I have changed, or things have changed around me because I’m not managing things the same way.\n\nDoctor: When you think specifically about the idea that dispatch and certain drivers are tracking and reporting on you, how distressing does that belief feel to you in the moment, on a scale from 0 to 10, where 0 is not upsetting at all and 10 is the most upset you can imagine?\n\nPatient: In the moment? It’s pretty high. I’d say… probably an 8 out of 10 most of the time, sometimes a 9 if it’s happening right then, like if I’ve just made a route change and then there’s some comment over the radio that feels pointed, or I notice one of the same drivers nearby again.\n\nIt’s not just annoying, it feels like my stomach drops, like I’m being watched and evaluated and I can’t just do my job normally. Even when I try to tell myself, okay, maybe this is just dispatch doing normal oversight, it doesn’t calm me down for long, because then I start thinking about the computer system and how they can see the route changes, and how the comments are said in this indirect way, and how certain drivers seem to know things or report back.\n\nSo yeah… I wouldn’t say it’s a 10 all day every day, because I can still talk to you and I can still try to reason through it. But when it feels active, when I feel like they’re tracking me or checking up on me, it’s very upsetting. Around an 8.\n\nDoctor: Before this situation at work started, have you ever had a period in your life when you were clearly out of touch with reality—like being convinced of something others said was impossible, or needing hospital care because of your thoughts or perceptions?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that kind of clear way, no.\n\nI mean, I’m trying to think back carefully because I don’t want to just say “no” too fast if there’s something I’m forgetting, but I can’t remember a time before this where people were saying, “That’s impossible,” and I was completely convinced anyway, or where things got to the point that I needed to be in a hospital because of what I was thinking or perceiving.\n\nI’ve had times where I was stressed or upset like anybody, but not… not something where I’d describe it as being clearly out of touch with reality. This work situation is the thing that feels different and hard to shake. And then later someone says something that connects to that same thing, like they already heard about it.\n\nSo yes, I can briefly consider that maybe I’m misunderstanding some of it. I do try. But it doesn’t really stay that way in my head, because it feels too specific. Even when people say, “That’s just normal oversight,” I still feel pretty sure that certain drivers are reporting back and that dispatch or compliance are tracking more than they admit.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your job, do you ever feel that people in other places—like neighbors, stores, or in public—are also watching you or sending you hidden messages about you?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean… I’m more on edge in public now, so if I’m at a store or in my building and somebody looks at me too long, I might notice it more than I used to. But I don’t have the same sense that the neighbors or random people at the grocery store are sending me messages.\n\nThe main thing is work. It’s dispatch and compliance, because they actually have the computer system and the route information, so that makes sense to me. They can see when I change something or when I’m not exactly where I’m expected to be, and then there’ll be those little comments on the radio that don’t say my name but feel like they’re aimed at me. And certain drivers being around at certain times, like they’re checking or reporting back—that’s what I keep seeing.\n\nSo outside of work, I might be more alert, I guess, but it doesn’t feel organized like that. It’s really tied to the job and the route tracking and the drivers.\n\nDoctor: Because of these concerns about dispatch and the other drivers, have you actually changed your behavior in any clear way—like avoiding certain routes, refusing assignments, or confronting people about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not always in a dramatic way like refusing everything, but yes, it has changed how I’m functioning. At work I’ve fallen behind on the route reports, which is embarrassing because I used to be pretty on top of those. I’ll sit there trying to write it up and then I start second-guessing what to include, or whether something I write is going to be used against me or picked apart, and then it just doesn’t get done the way it should.\n\nAnd my instructions to drivers have gotten… I guess confusing. I don’t mean to make them confusing, but I’ll be trying to be careful with how I word things, or I’ll change how I explain a route because I’m thinking about who might be listening or what they’re going to say about it later. Then the drivers complain because they’re like, “What exactly are you telling me to do?” or they say I wasn’t clear. And then that just makes me feel worse, because it feels like it gives people more reason to watch me.\n\nAt home it’s been showing up too. My husband has had to take over a lot of the normal stuff—meals, phone calls, basic planning things—because I’m not keeping up with it. Like I’ll mean to call someone back or figure out dinner or handle some simple errand, and then I get stuck thinking through what happened at work or trying to sort out what it all means, and hours go by. So yes, I guess I have changed, or things have changed around me because I’m not managing things the same way.\n\nDoctor: When you think specifically about the idea that dispatch and certain drivers are tracking and reporting on you, how distressing does that belief feel to you in the moment, on a scale from 0 to 10, where 0 is not upsetting at all and 10 is the most upset you can imagine?\n\nPatient: In the moment? It’s pretty high. I’d say… probably an 8 out of 10 most of the time, sometimes a 9 if it’s happening right then, like if I’ve just made a route change and then there’s some comment over the radio that feels pointed, or I notice one of the same drivers nearby again.\n\nIt’s not just annoying, it feels like my stomach drops, like I’m being watched and evaluated and I can’t just do my job normally. Even when I try to tell myself, okay, maybe this is just dispatch doing normal oversight, it doesn’t calm me down for long, because then I start thinking about the computer system and how they can see the route changes, and how the comments are said in this indirect way, and how certain drivers seem to know things or report back.\n\nSo yeah… I wouldn’t say it’s a 10 all day every day, because I can still talk to you and I can still try to reason through it. But when it feels active, when I feel like they’re tracking me or checking up on me, it’s very upsetting. Around an 8.\n\nDoctor: Before this situation at work started, have you ever had a period in your life when you were clearly out of touch with reality—like being convinced of something others said was impossible, or needing hospital care because of your thoughts or perceptions?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that kind of clear way, no.\n\nI mean, I’m trying to think back carefully because I don’t want to just say “no” too fast if there’s something I’m forgetting, but I can’t remember a time before this where people were saying, “That’s impossible,” and I was completely convinced anyway, or where things got to the point that I needed to be in a hospital because of what I was thinking or perceiving.\n\nI’ve had times where I was stressed or upset like anybody, but not… not something where I’d describe it as being clearly out of touch with reality. This work situation is the thing that feels different and hard to shake. Before that, I don’t think there was anything like what you’re asking about.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… sitting here, I know you’re asking if there’s a part of me that can doubt it, and I guess there is some tiny part that knows other people keep calling it “normal oversight” or saying dispatch just has to monitor route changes because that’s their job. I can say that out loud. I understand that’s what people are telling me.

But it doesn’t really feel uncertain to me, not in the way I think you mean. It feels like it’s happening. Like, I’m here because dispatch and compliance have been tracking my route changes through the computer system, and then these little comments come over the radio that line up too closely with what I just did. And certain drivers… it’s the same ones, they seem to know where I’ve been or what I changed, and then I get the sense they’re reporting back.

So if you ask me, “Is it absolutely true?” I don’t know if I want to say absolutely, because then it sounds like I’m being unreasonable or like I can’t consider anything else. But in my body, in my head when I picture it, it feels true. It feels more true than the explanation that it’s all just routine. I can try to leave room for maybe I’m missing something, but that room is small. Most of me thinks they are tracking me and reporting on me.

  {

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23, F20.0, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… honestly, it’s been getting hard to keep pretending this is just normal work stuff. I work in transportation, and dispatch and compliance have been watching my route changes through the computer system. Like, when I adjust something or take a different turn, it’s like they already know and then there’ll be these little comments on the radio—not direct enough that someone else would catch it, maybe, but I can tell it’s about me.\n\nPeople keep saying, “That’s just oversight,” or “They track everyone,” and I understand that there’s a system for routes and schedules and all that. I’m not stupid. But this feels different. It’s the way certain drivers will say things or suddenly be around, and I’m pretty sure some of them are reporting on me. I just… I need someone to take it seriously, because it’s making it really hard to do my job like normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or sounds that other people around you don’t seem to hear, when there’s no radio or other obvious source?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say it like that exactly. I mean, at work there’s always noise—radios, engines, doors, people talking over each other—so sometimes I’ll hear something and not be sure where it came from. But when you say “voices with no radio or obvious source,” I’m not sure I’ve really had that in a clear way.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll notice comments or sounds and then I look around and other people act like they didn’t hear it or they pretend they didn’t. So it’s hard for me to answer, because I don’t always know if they truly didn’t hear it, or if they’re just acting like it wasn’t said. But no, I don’t think I’ve had, like, a voice in an empty room talking to me, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: When you think about dispatch and the other drivers watching and commenting on you, how sure are you that this is really happening, on a scale from 0 to 100 percent?\n\nPatient: I mean… if I’m being completely honest, like 85, maybe 90 percent sure. I know that sounds high, and I know people will say, “Well, dispatch has to know where you are, that’s their job,” and yes, I get that. I’m not saying the whole system shouldn’t exist or that nobody else gets tracked. We all have routes and logs and times and everything goes through the computer.\n\nBut the part that makes me so sure is how it lines up. If I change something on my route, or if I’m thinking I’m going to avoid a certain street, then suddenly there’s a radio comment that’s not saying my name, but it’s worded in this way where I know it’s about me. Or another driver will be in just the right place, like they’re checking whether I did what I was supposed to do. And then later someone says something casual that connects back to it.\n\nSo… yeah, I leave a little room because I’m trying to be reasonable, and I know I can’t prove every single piece of it. But it doesn’t feel like a coincidence. It feels like dispatch and compliance are watching the route changes and certain drivers are feeding things back. So I’d say around 90 percent.\n\nDoctor: In the past few weeks, have you been feeling noticeably down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not “most of the day, nearly every day,” no. I mean, I’ve been stressed and worn down, definitely, and there are days where I come home and I just feel heavy because I’m replaying everything that happened at work and wondering who said what or what it meant. But if you mean depressed like just sad all day, every day, I don’t know that I’d call it that.\n\nIt’s more like I’m tense and frustrated, and sometimes exhausted from trying to stay on top of things. There are moments where I feel pretty low, especially when I feel like no one believes me or they brush it off like I’m overreacting. But it’s not like I wake up every single day feeling depressed from morning to night. At least, I don’t think that’s the main thing.\n\nDoctor: When someone you trust suggests that these things at work might be coincidences or misunderstandings, are you ever able to seriously consider that possibility, even briefly, or does it still feel definitely true to you no matter what they say?\n\nPatient: I can consider it for a minute, especially if it’s someone I trust and they’re not saying it in a mocking way. Like, I’ll try to step back and tell myself, okay, maybe dispatch is just doing what dispatch does, maybe compliance is just looking at the computer because that’s normal for routes and timing and all that. I’m not unreasonable—I know there’s oversight in transportation, and I know not every radio comment is automatically about me.\n\nBut then when I go back over the details, it stops feeling like coincidence. It’s the pattern of it. Like if I change a route or I do something slightly different, and then there’s a comment on the radio that’s phrased just vaguely enough, but it fits exactly with what I did. Or certain drivers show up in places where it feels like they’re checking on whether I followed the route. And then later someone says something that connects to that same thing, like they already heard about it.\n\nSo yes, I can briefly consider that maybe I’m misunderstanding some of it. I do try. But it doesn’t really stay that way in my head, because it feels too specific. Even when people say, “That’s just normal oversight,” I still feel pretty sure that certain drivers are reporting back and that dispatch or compliance are tracking more than they admit.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your job, do you ever feel that people in other places—like neighbors, stores, or in public—are also watching you or sending you hidden messages about you?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean… I’m more on edge in public now, so if I’m at a store or in my building and somebody looks at me too long, I might notice it more than I used to. But I don’t have the same sense that the neighbors or random people at the grocery store are sending me messages.\n\nThe main thing is work. It’s dispatch and compliance, because they actually have the computer system and the route information, so that makes sense to me. They can see when I change something or when I’m not exactly where I’m expected to be, and then there’ll be those little comments on the radio that don’t say my name but feel like they’re aimed at me. And certain drivers being around at certain times, like they’re checking or reporting back—that’s what I keep seeing.\n\nSo outside of work, I might be more alert, I guess, but it doesn’t feel organized like that. It’s really tied to the job and the route tracking and the drivers.\n\nDoctor: Because of these concerns about dispatch and the other drivers, have you actually changed your behavior in any clear way—like avoiding certain routes, refusing assignments, or confronting people about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not always in a dramatic way like refusing everything, but yes, it has changed how I’m functioning. At work I’ve fallen behind on the route reports, which is embarrassing because I used to be pretty on top of those. I’ll sit there trying to write it up and then I start second-guessing what to include, or whether something I write is going to be used against me or picked apart, and then it just doesn’t get done the way it should.\n\nAnd my instructions to drivers have gotten… I guess confusing. I don’t mean to make them confusing, but I’ll be trying to be careful with how I word things, or I’ll change how I explain a route because I’m thinking about who might be listening or what they’re going to say about it later. Then the drivers complain because they’re like, “What exactly are you telling me to do?” or they say I wasn’t clear. And then that just makes me feel worse, because it feels like it gives people more reason to watch me.\n\nAt home it’s been showing up too. My husband has had to take over a lot of the normal stuff—meals, phone calls, basic planning things—because I’m not keeping up with it. Like I’ll mean to call someone back or figure out dinner or handle some simple errand, and then I get stuck thinking through what happened at work or trying to sort out what it all means, and hours go by. So yes, I guess I have changed, or things have changed around me because I’m not managing things the same way.\n\nDoctor: When you think specifically about the idea that dispatch and certain drivers are tracking and reporting on you, how distressing does that belief feel to you in the moment, on a scale from 0 to 10, where 0 is not upsetting at all and 10 is the most upset you can imagine?\n\nPatient: In the moment? It’s pretty high. I’d say… probably an 8 out of 10 most of the time, sometimes a 9 if it’s happening right then, like if I’ve just made a route change and then there’s some comment over the radio that feels pointed, or I notice one of the same drivers nearby again.\n\nIt’s not just annoying, it feels like my stomach drops, like I’m being watched and evaluated and I can’t just do my job normally. Even when I try to tell myself, okay, maybe this is just dispatch doing normal oversight, it doesn’t calm me down for long, because then I start thinking about the computer system and how they can see the route changes, and how the comments are said in this indirect way, and how certain drivers seem to know things or report back.\n\nSo yeah… I wouldn’t say it’s a 10 all day every day, because I can still talk to you and I can still try to reason through it. But when it feels active, when I feel like they’re tracking me or checking up on me, it’s very upsetting. Around an 8.\n\nDoctor: Before this situation at work started, have you ever had a period in your life when you were clearly out of touch with reality—like being convinced of something others said was impossible, or needing hospital care because of your thoughts or perceptions?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that kind of clear way, no.\n\nI mean, I’m trying to think back carefully because I don’t want to just say “no” too fast if there’s something I’m forgetting, but I can’t remember a time before this where people were saying, “That’s impossible,” and I was completely convinced anyway, or where things got to the point that I needed to be in a hospital because of what I was thinking or perceiving.\n\nI’ve had times where I was stressed or upset like anybody, but not… not something where I’d describe it as being clearly out of touch with reality. This work situation is the thing that feels different and hard to shake. Before that, I don’t think there was anything like what you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Right now, as you’re sitting here with me, if you focus just on the idea that dispatch and certain drivers are tracking and reporting on you, does that feel absolutely true to you, or does any part of you currently feel unsure that it’s really happening?\n\nPatient: I mean… sitting here, I know you’re asking if there’s a part of me that can doubt it, and I guess there is some tiny part that knows other people keep calling it “normal oversight” or saying dispatch just has to monitor route changes because that’s their job. I can say that out loud. I understand that’s what people are telling me.\n\nBut it doesn’t really feel uncertain to me, not in the way I think you mean. It feels like it’s happening. I can try to leave room for maybe I’m missing something, but that room is small. Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli. Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger.
